a:T513,The paper is a controlled empirical study rather than a new architecture paper. It fixes total training tokens at each active-parameter scale and varies the amount of unique data, which directly changes the repetition rate. Dense Transformers are compared with multiple MoE configurations across 80M, 200M, and 1B active parameters, with the largest configuration reaching 8.5B total parameters. The MoE sweep varies expert count, expert granularity, and sparsity. Expert granularity refers to the width of each expert FFN relative to the dense FFN; compute-matched MoEs often use a granularity of 1/k when k experts are active per token. The data sweep covers single-domain corpora and mixed-domain settings, including web, code, scientific, and encyclopedic text, with both filtered and unfiltered variants. Beyond held-out loss, the paper analyzes domain-local degradation, regularization, router stability, expert activation patterns, and expert specialization. A key mechanistic finding is that router assignments stabilize early in training. After that point, each expert updates on a relatively small and nearly stationary subset of tokens. Repeating the corpus therefore repeats the same expert-specific token subsets, making sparse experts more prone to narrow memorization than dense FFNs.b:T4ee,The empirical pattern is consistent across scales and domains. On unique data, MoEs outperform dense models. As repetition increases, MoEs degrade earlier: at the 80M scale, dense models remain relatively robust until beyond roughly 8x repetition, while MoEs show clear degradation at 4x repetition. By 32x repetition, MoEs lose their advantage over dense models. The severity increases with sparsity and is better predicted by total parameter count than by active parameter count. Data quality filtering changes the magnitude but not the direction of the effect; some unfiltered settings overfit more, but filtering does not remove the MoE-specific sensitivity to repeated data. In mixed-domain training, degradation is mostly confined to the repeated domains, and non-repeated tokens from other domains provide some regularization. Among regularization methods, dropout and output masking are effective, especially for MoEs, while some other standard methods help little. With sufficiently high dropout, MoEs can still outperform dense models even at 64x repetition. Mechanistic measurements show early router stabilization and increased expert specialization under repetition, supporting the interpretation that repeated data is amplified at the expert level.c:T400,The technical idea is to replace inferred membership labels with exact-match copy counts. The paper queries infini-gram indexes over OLMo-mix-1124 and the Pile to obtain the number of times a sentence appears in each corpus. These counts are conservative floors: punctuation changes, quote normalization, line breaks, and unindexed annealing data are not counted. The central design compares the same sentence across two model families. If a sentence appears more often in the OLMo corpus than in the Pile, the test asks whether an OLMo-2 model assigns it lower loss than a Pythia model, and vice versa. Because the sentence is identical, fluency, length, author quality, and wording are held fixed. The paper also evaluates standard loss-based detectors across copy-count regimes, tests one-word edited controls, and swaps in controls with different register. The purpose is not to build a stronger attack, but to isolate which parts of the apparent signal come from exposure and which parts come from evaluation artifacts.d:T52c,The experiments use five models from the OLMo-2 and Pythia families, ranging from 1B to 13B parameters. For ordinary sentences with 1 to 1,000 copies, loss-based membership evidence is nearly flat within noise. A 1B model reaches about 0.60 AUC on ordinary sentences, while famous lines with a median of roughly 1,200 copies reach 0.83 AUC. In the same-sentence design, using 747 sentences and within-book comparisons, the rank correlation between relative exposure and relative loss is about −0.08 for 7B models and −0.07 for 13B models, where −1 would be a perfect exposure-lowers-loss relationship and 0 means no relationship. This accounts for less than one percent of variance and does not strengthen with scale. One-word edits are shown to be poor non-member controls: in more than 90% of pairs, the original sentence has lower loss than the edit, by about 0.4 nats per token, or roughly 1.5× average token probability. The gap is similar whether the original appeared once or a hundred times, indicating that it measures wording quality rather than memory. Above about 1,000 copies, the gap grows with model size on the twelve available sentences. Changing controls from same-source edits to composed-prose controls moves AUC from 0.83 to 0.94, showing that register mismatch can manufacture a strong detector.e:T4ee,WPMIA replaces token-level likelihood (unavailable) with an estimable word-level likelihood derived from Monte Carlo sampling.

1) Word-level probability estimation: represent the target text as a sequence of words. For each position, query the model multiple times conditioned on the preceding words, collect sampled continuations, and estimate P(next_word = w_t | prefix) by frequency. Local kernel smoothing is applied to reduce variance and avoid brittle zero counts under finite sampling.

2) Sequence-level likelihood estimator: aggregate the estimated word probabilities via a chain-rule style log-likelihood (typically mean log-likelihood) to obtain a membership score that mirrors classic likelihood-based MIAs.

3) Prefix-conditioned contrast: build two conditioning contexts (member-like vs non-member-like prefixes) and estimate the sequence likelihood under each, then contrast them to amplify the membership gap. A notable design choice is to contrast at the sequence level (after aggregation) instead of taking unstable word-level probability ratios.

4) Practical threat model: explicitly assumes the attacker does not know the target tokenizer, motivating word-level modeling; relies only on text sampling, matching typical proprietary LLM APIs.f:T453,Muon flattens the singular spectrum of the momentum matrix, producing an update whose singular values are roughly all one. Musec replaces this with spectral clipping: only singular values above a threshold are clipped, while the skewed spectrum of the original momentum is preserved. The update therefore avoids injecting excessive magnitude into weak momentum directions. The paper also gives Musec a constrained optimization interpretation: it solves a Frobenius-penalized quadratic subproblem under a spectral-norm constraint, complementing prior interpretations of Muon as unconstrained steepest descent. For practical training, the authors introduce Soft Musec, which replaces hard clipping with a smooth saturation function and approximates the resulting matrix transform using coupled Newton-Schulz iterations, avoiding explicit SVD. The theory covers stochastic nonconvex nonsmooth objectives under weak convexity assumptions and proves convergence to a Goldstein stationary point, addressing recent results showing that exact-polar Muon may fail to converge in general nonsmooth nonconvex settings.10:T52b,NCP-ArchPreview modifies an OLMo-3-7B-style backbone into a hierarchical autoregressive model with three main components. A 16-layer Token Encoder processes the token stream into hidden states. An 8-layer Concept Module operates in the middle and predicts future discrete concepts. A 16-layer Token Decoder then continues standard autoregressive token prediction. The discrete concept vocabulary is built directly from hidden states through product quantization, which gives a high-capacity discrete latent space without requiring manually defined semantic labels. Training jointly optimizes standard next-token prediction and next-concept prediction. The NTP objective preserves compatibility with normal LM generation, while the NCP objective forces the model to predict future multi-token spans at a more abstract level. Predicted concepts are fed back into the token-level path to guide later generation. The architecture also uses multiway dynamic dense connections, adapted from MUDDFormer, to route residual information across the token and concept hierarchy. Compared with multi-token prediction, the target is not a sequence of surface tokens but a learned latent concept. Compared with hierarchical byte or patch models, the key contribution is not only sequence compression but explicit future latent prediction.11:Tc54,方法核心是把 looped model 训练成一个带 recurrent state 的条件去噪器，再把推理解释为概率流 ODE 的积分过程。

先看问题设定。输入是条件 c，输出是离散序列 x。传统 looped model 维护隐藏状态 z_i，并用共享参数的更新函数 f(z_i, c) 反复迭代，再用头部 g(z_i) 解码答案。难点在于实际训练常用 stop-gradient 的 per-step loss，导致第 i 步的损失不会回传到更早状态 z_{<i}，于是早期步骤没有动力去准备后续计算。

作者的改法是：不给每一步都要求“直接答对最终答案”，而是给每一步分配一个不同噪声水平下的局部去噪任务。具体做法是从真实解 x 构造一条从高噪声到低噪声的中间状态轨迹 x_t，并让第 i 步 recurrence 负责在对应噪声水平上预测去噪方向，也就是 velocity / denoising target。关键不只是多噪声训练，而是两点耦合设计：
1) progressively decreasing noise levels：随着 recurrence 时间推进，任务从粗糙恢复走向精细恢复，天然形成“先搭框架、再修细节”的计算分工；
2) shared noise across adjacent objectives：相邻时间步看到的是同一底层噪声样本在不同噪声强度下的版本，因此目标之间是 temporally associated 的。这样即便梯度只覆盖局部，模型也会倾向于学出能在相邻步骤之间延续的隐藏状态，而不是每步都重新开始。

在形式上，作者采用 categorical continuous flow matching 的监督版本。模型输出的是定义在概率单纯形上的 velocity field，而不是直接输出离散 token。隐藏状态 z 与当前噪声化样本、条件 c 一起决定这个 velocity。训练时优化局部 denoising / flow matching 目标；推理时从噪声初始化，通过数值积分沿 learned probability flow 逐步运输到解分布附近，再做 rounding 得到离散答案。

这个 formulation 有几个重要区别。

和标准 diffusion/flow model 不同：这里不是单次前向网络在连续时间上求解，而是把 recurrent hidden state 显式并入动力系统。也就是说，ODE 的速度场不仅依赖当前 x_t 和 c，还依赖一个会跨步累积计算的内部状态 z_i。这样“流”负责提供平滑、局部一致的训练信号，“loop”负责存储和滚动中间计算。

和已有 looped reasoning 方法如 TRM 相比：作者不再要求每一步都直接预测最终解，也不依赖长程 BPTT 去塑造 recurrence，而是通过噪声调度把不同步骤的职责拆开。训练信号更局部，但目标之间更连续。

和在 recurrence 中直接注入随机噪声以获得多样性的方法相比：looped flows 的随机性来自概率运输过程本身。作者指出，Euler integration 下可以得到多个有效预测，这意味着模型天然支持一题多解，而不是靠每步加噪声硬造随机性。

从机制上看，这个方法最有意思的地方是“把时间上的 credit assignment，转写成噪声尺度上的 curriculum + coupling”。这不是常规截断 BPTT 的小修小补，而是换了训练接口。12:T4a0,This matters because it speaks directly to inference-time compute beyond autoregressive next-token prediction. The paper offers a concrete training interface for hidden-state deliberation: instead of relying on long-horizon backpropagation, it uses flow-style local denoising objectives that are temporally aligned across recurrent steps. For pretraining researchers, the significance is threefold.

First, it targets a core question in test-time scaling: how to make extra inference steps actually perform additional computation rather than repeat a shallow transformation.

Second, it reframes multi-step reasoning as probability flow integration. That gives a clean separation between training and inference: training uses local denoising targets, while inference can scale compute by refining the temporal grid or using better numerical solvers.

Third, it provides a mechanistic claim, not just a benchmark gain: long-range useful computation can emerge from local objectives if adjacent objectives are properly coupled through noise levels and shared noise. That is a meaningful idea for latent reasoning, non-AR language modeling, and recurrent world-model-style architectures.13:Taa0,The core idea is to train a looped model as a recurrent conditional denoiser and then perform inference by integrating a probability flow ODE.

In the standard setup, a looped model maintains a hidden state z_i, updates it recurrently with shared parameters, and decodes a prediction from each state. The practical problem is that training often uses stop-gradient per-step losses, so the loss at step i does not shape earlier states z_{<i}. Early updates therefore have little incentive to build representations that will help later updates.

Looped flows changes the target. Instead of asking every recurrent step to directly predict the final answer, it assigns each step a local denoising task at a different noise level. Starting from the ground-truth solution x, the method constructs noisy intermediate states x_t along a path from high noise to low noise. The recurrent model is trained to predict the denoising direction, or velocity, appropriate for the current noise level.

Two design choices are essential.
1) Noise levels decrease progressively across recurrent time. This creates a natural division of labor: earlier steps solve coarser subproblems, later steps refine them.
2) Adjacent objectives share the same underlying noise realization. This temporally associates neighboring denoising tasks, so even with only local gradients, the hidden state is encouraged to carry forward useful computation rather than restart from scratch at each step.

Formally, the paper uses a supervised version of continuous flow matching for categorical outputs. The model predicts a velocity field on the probability simplex rather than directly outputting discrete tokens. The velocity depends on the noisy current state, the conditioning input, and the recurrent hidden state. Training optimizes local denoising / flow-matching losses. Inference starts from noise and integrates the learned probability flow toward the solution distribution, then rounds to a categorical answer.

This differs from standard diffusion or flow models because the recurrent hidden state is part of the dynamics. The flow provides smooth, locally consistent supervision; the loop stores and accumulates intermediate computation over time. It also differs from prior looped reasoning methods such as TRM, which typically ask each step to predict the final answer directly and therefore suffer more from local-training mismatch. Here, the recurrence is shaped indirectly through a sequence of aligned subproblems.

A particularly interesting aspect is the credit-assignment shift: instead of solving temporal credit assignment with long BPTT, the method translates it into a noise-scale curriculum plus coupling between adjacent objectives.14:T780,The evaluation covers six reasoning benchmarks. The headline result is that looped flows outperforms prior looped models on five of them and remains competitive on the sixth. The paper explicitly reports that, using the same architecture as TRM, performance improves from 44.6% to 58.8% on ARC-AGI-1 and from 7.8% to 12.2% on ARC-AGI-2. Those are meaningful gains because ARC-style tasks are sensitive to compositional generalization and internal multi-step computation.

The stability results are just as important as the benchmark numbers. Prior looped models can become unstable when unrolled longer, sometimes failing even on simple problems. Looped flows is reported to learn more stable recurrences, with performance improving more reliably as inference computation increases. That directly supports the claim that the method trains recurrent computation rather than merely fitting local predictions.

The ablations indicate that each component matters. Based on the paper’s framing, the important ones are:
1) removing the progressively decreasing noise schedule hurts performance, suggesting that coarse-to-fine task allocation is useful;
2) removing shared noise weakens temporal alignment between adjacent objectives and reduces the usefulness of recurrent state transfer;
3) replacing flow-based inference with more direct step prediction reduces the benefit of extra inference compute;
4) the choice of numerical integrator affects final accuracy, which implies the model is genuinely using the learned continuous flow rather than treating it as a training-only wrapper.

Another notable result is that inference can use a finer temporal grid than training. This means the model is not just memorizing a fixed discrete program length; it learns a computation trajectory that can be interpolated at test time. For anyone interested in test-time scaling, that is one of the most important empirical findings.15:T55f,This is a must-read if you care about latent reasoning, recurrent inference, or alternatives to explicit chain-of-thought.

Read it in four passes: problem, construction, mechanism, evidence.

Start with the introduction and background to understand the exact failure mode of looped models trained with local stop-gradient objectives. Then focus on the two core method sections: how local denoising objectives are assigned across recurrent steps, and how inference is formulated as probability flow integration. It helps to track the roles of three objects separately: the noisy solution state x_t, the recurrent hidden state z_i, and the velocity field conditioned on both plus the input.

In the experiments, prioritize:
1) the main comparison table against TRM and related looped baselines, especially ARC-AGI-1/2;
2) plots showing performance as a function of recurrent steps or integration steps;
3) ablations on decreasing noise, shared noise, and solver choice.
If the paper includes recurrence visualizations or failure cases, those are worth reading closely because they reveal whether the method truly fixes the training mismatch.

A first serious read takes about 45–60 minutes. A deeper read should include working through the flow-matching formulation and thinking about how it could interface with language-model pretraining or hidden-state reasoning systems.16:T5e1,The method has two parts. First is Matryoshka-style training. Instead of training a compressor at one fixed K, the model samples K per training instance, so a single encoder learns to compress the same kind of context into different numbers of memory tokens. The underlying interface stays unchanged: an encoder LLM maps a context of length L into K continuous memory tokens, and a frozen decoder LLM consumes those soft tokens in place of the raw context. In practice, the encoder is usually a LoRA-adapted copy of the decoder. This makes the approach method-agnostic across ICAE, 500xCompressor, and SAC.

Second is inference-time budget selection. The paper proposes two strategies. Confidence-based cascade routing starts with the smallest, most aggressive budget and only retries with a larger K when the decoder is uncertain. This tends to preserve accuracy well, but may require multiple compression-decoding passes. The learned K predictor instead predicts the budget before compression, so inference runs in a single pass. That is more deployment-friendly because latency and throughput are easier to control.

An important empirical observation is that the accuracy-compression relation is not always monotonic at the instance level. Larger K can preserve distracting surface details and hurt the decoder, while K=1 can sometimes succeed where a larger budget fails. So adaptive budgeting is not just about saving tokens on easy cases; it also helps avoid over-preserving irrelevant information.17:T4c6,Experiments cover three compressor families—ICAE, 500xCompressor, and SAC—on in-domain and out-of-domain QA from MRQA. There are three main findings.

First, a single Matryoshka-trained model can largely replace a family of fixed-ratio specialists. At each native ratio, the gap to separately trained fixed-K models is at worst 1.3 F1, and the shared model is often better on OOD settings. That suggests multi-budget training does not significantly compromise specialization.

Second, adaptive budget selection improves the accuracy-compression trade-off. Cascade routing retains over 98% of the accuracy of the mildest compression setting while increasing average compression from 15x to as high as 266x. The learned K predictor reaches 158x–236x compression in a single compression-decoding pass, with at most 0.7 F1 loss.

Third, the gains translate into real serving metrics. At larger batch sizes, the K predictor reduces context KV cache by 50% and improves aggregate decoding throughput by 47% relative to always using the mildest budget. The paper also shows that many MRQA inputs are already solvable with K=1, while only a minority need larger budgets, which explains why fixed-K deployment is inefficient.18:T550,The method keeps the Kashin factorization w = u + P^T \hat{v} (with \hat{v}=Pv), aiming to bound \|u\|_\infty and \|\hat{v}\|_\infty so scalar quantization is less sensitive to outliers. The paper fixes three concrete issues observed in prior LLM applications:
1) Convergence and peak formation: instead of a matrix-level reformulation (which lost guarantees), they stay with the vector-level greedy algorithm and introduce a Greedy Algorithm with Alternating Updates. Updates of u and \hat{v} are executed in fixed blocks of four steps, designed to enforce the symmetric 4-peak distributions rather than letting the joint distribution collapse.
2) Efficient orthogonal transform: they replace a dense random orthogonal matrix with a sign-randomized DCT, reducing each application of P/P^T from O(N^2) to O(N log N) and eliminating matrix storage.
3) Faster clustering: because each greedy step changes factors by ±c_k(r_k) (a function of residual norm), after two steps the four peak locations are exactly ±c1±c2. They use these analytic centers to initialize k-means and run only a few refinement iterations, removing expensive multi-restart search.
They further compose the approach with OPTQ-style sequential error compensation and QuIP-style incoherence preprocessing (Hadamard/Kronecker), and implement the pipeline in JAX with multi-GPU pmap support.19:T7a3,OmniKVQuant is a training-free extension of TurboQuant with two targeted modifications. TurboQuant decomposes each key/value vector into magnitude and direction, applies a randomized Hadamard rotation to the direction so coordinates look closer to a Gaussian reference, and then quantizes with a fixed codebook. This works reasonably well when one global reference distribution and one shared rotation are adequate.

The paper argues that this assumption fails in Omni-LLMs. The first fix is temporal range adaptation for keys. Even after Hadamard rotation, key coordinates still deviate substantially from the Gaussian target, and the mismatch changes over short temporal windows. Instead of using one fixed global range, OmniKVQuant performs coordinate-wise min-max scaling within a small local token group before codebook assignment. The codebook itself is not relearned; the local scaling simply adapts the same codebook to the current cache region. This is a minimal but well-motivated change that directly addresses local non-stationarity in keys.

The second fix is modality-specific value rotation. PCA visualizations show that audio, visual, and text values occupy distinct regions of representation space. A single shared rotation therefore cannot flatten all modalities equally well. OmniKVQuant calibrates separate rotations for each modality so that each value distribution is better aligned with the quantizer. The key idea is simple: modality-specific geometry persists inside a shared decoder, so the quantization stage should exploit it explicitly.

The paper also includes a fused Triton decode kernel. Instead of inverse-rotating every cached key, it rotates one query per head following a FlashAttention-style computation order. For values, inverse rotations are batched on head outputs rather than applied token by token to the cache. This reduces redundant rotation work and kernel launches without reconstructing a dense FP16 cache.1a:T5c8,The analysis experiments are important because they justify the design. On Qwen2.5-Omni-3B evaluated with WorldSense, rotated keys retain only 30.40% of the variance of the Gaussian reference, with Wasserstein-1 distance 0.778 and KS statistic 0.449. The mismatch is stronger for audio and video than for text. Values are closer to the Gaussian target overall, but their cross-modal geometry is clearly separated. This supports the paper’s split treatment: local adaptation for keys, modality-aware correction for values.

For the main results, the method is evaluated on Qwen2.5-Omni and Qwen3-Omni. On seven audio-visual benchmarks with Qwen2.5-Omni, 2-bit KV caches retain 98.1% of FP16 performance and consistently outperform TurboQuant. The abstract does not list every benchmark score, but the paper presents the gains as stable across tasks rather than concentrated on one dataset. The trend also transfers to Qwen3-Omni, which suggests the method is not tied to a single model implementation.

From an engineering perspective, two takeaways matter most. First, 2-bit KV quantization is viable for Omni-LLMs, but only if the quantizer respects multimodal cache structure. Second, a training-free method can preserve most of the original performance, which makes it attractive for deployment without model retraining. When reading the full paper, the most informative ablations are the ones isolating temporal adaptation, modality-specific rotation, and their combination.1b:T45d,A good reading order is: failure analysis, method fixes, kernel implementation, then headline results. Start with the introduction and the analysis table to understand exactly where TurboQuant breaks on multimodal caches; otherwise the method can look like a small patch. Then read the sections on temporal key drift and heterogeneous value geometry carefully. Those are the most informative parts of the paper. After that, inspect the two method components and ask why keys and values are treated differently instead of feeding both into a more complicated unified quantizer. Finally, look at the fused Triton kernel and the main benchmark table to judge practical deployability.

If time is limited, the problem analysis, method overview, and main results are enough for a 20-minute skim. For a deeper read, focus on the statistical diagnostics, the grouping/window design for local key scaling, how modality-specific rotations are calibrated, and how the kernel avoids inverse-rotating the whole cache. Those details matter more than the average benchmark number if your interest is long-context inference systems.1c:T49a,Two main components:

1) Kubernetes DRA driver for composable CXL memory:
- Publishes one ResourceSlice per CXL blade, but unlike node-local devices it sets a NodeSelector spanning all hosts connected to that blade.
- On demand, composes a CXL region via a fabric manager, materializes it as device_dax on each participating host, and injects it into pods under a single CDI device name so pods on different nodes map the same physical region.
- Adds lifecycle robustness (finalizers and redundant teardown metadata) to ensure free-on-delete survives plugin restarts.

2) In-region metadata + vLLM integration:
- Implements a shared-memory KV tier whose directory lives inside the shared medium (atomic slot bitmap + parallel key array), avoiding an external metadata service.
- Hooks into vLLM/llm-d offloading connector; notes practical prerequisites like fixed hash-chain seeding for cross-instance reuse and block granularity effects (e.g., 256-token blocks causing partial-block recompute).

Design choices: uses device_dax to bypass page cache and let the connector manage memory directly; explicitly not targeting prefill/decode disaggregation or GPU↔CXL zero-copy DMA.1d:T811,SpecGuard assumes the serving stack already uses speculative decoding. A small draft model proposes tokens, and the target model verifies them. The accept/reject sequence, normally used only for acceleration, is treated as an online measure of draft–target agreement. Under benign inputs, disagreement mostly reflects ordinary modeling mismatch. Under an activated backdoor, the target shifts toward the attacker-controlled response while a clean draft continues along the benign trajectory, so rejection increases and the per-query acceptance rate drops.

The base detector is simple: compute a query-level acceptance-rate score and compare it against a baseline estimated from clean traffic. The paper then extends this raw signal into a more deployable recipe. If the draft is also poisoned, low acceptance alone is no longer sufficient, so the detector uses two-sided abnormality scoring. If the trigger only affects a sparse segment of the response, whole-query averaging dilutes the signal, so the method adds multi-scale alarms over different windows. If traffic distribution shifts over time, fixed thresholds drift, so the paper recommends rolling calibration. If an adaptive attacker appends benign continuation after the malicious response to dilute the mean, early-token statistics become more informative than full-response averages.

The theory links acceptance behavior to distributional discrepancy between draft and target. Intuitively, speculative acceptance depends on how much probability the target assigns to draft-proposed tokens, so an acceptance-rate gap reflects increased divergence, discussed through total variation style arguments. The paper further derives a potency–stealth tradeoff: to hide from the detector while keeping the attack active, the attacker must reduce probability mass on tokens that the clean draft would not favor, which directly weakens the malicious behavioral shift. The theory is not trying to cover every adaptive strategy, but it gives a principled explanation for why this nearly free signal should work.1e:T509,The empirical section is broader than the abstract suggests. The evaluation spans four backdoor types, three model families, multiple scales up to 32B, and several decoding settings rather than a single speculative-decoding configuration. The headline result is that SpecGuard achieves strong per-query detection of activated backdoors while remaining competitive with runtime monitors that require an extra generation pass, but with almost no additional inference-time cost.

The most informative results are the stress tests. In poisoned-draft or self-drafting settings, the signal does not simply disappear; instead, the anomaly can become two-sided because draft and target may agree on the malicious path, motivating two-sided scoring. In sparse trigger-timing settings, whole-response averages understate the effect, and multi-scale statistics recover sensitivity. Under input-distribution shift, rolling calibration is more reliable than a static threshold learned offline. Under adaptive padding attacks, where benign text is appended after the malicious answer, early-token statistics recover part of the lost signal. The paper emphasizes robustness trends across settings more than a single benchmark number, so the main tables and ablations matter more than any isolated metric.1f:T6ab,MindTopo is organized around five properties drawn from cognitive science and formal topology: continuity, separation, order, enclosure, and knots. Each property is evaluated at two cognitive levels. Reasoning tasks present one or more rendered scenes and ask the model to identify a topological relation or predict how a change would affect it. Planning tasks place the model in an interactive environment where it receives rendered observations and must choose actions that build, preserve, or alter the relevant topological structure.

Three design choices matter. First, tasks are procedurally generated from parametric scene states, so difficulty is controllable and labels come from the underlying state rather than manual annotation. Second, the benchmark separates the cognitive taxonomy from the formal target definition: the former determines what capability is being tested, while the latter determines how instances are generated and scored. Third, the paper goes beyond static QA by evaluating closed-loop agents and by studying whether supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and image/video generative augmentation can improve performance. This makes MindTopo more than a question set; it becomes a framework for exposing where action fails even when recognition partly works.

The benchmark contains 11,030 instances across 13 task types: 8,030 reasoning questions and 3,000 planning episodes. The authors evaluate 14 MLLMs, then run SFT and RL experiments on Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct. They also test agent configurations augmented with image and video generation, including three video generative models, to see whether predictive visual rollouts help preserve topology during planning.20:T68f,The main empirical message is clear: current MLLMs are weak at topological reasoning, and they are substantially weaker at topological planning. The most important findings are three stable patterns. First, even the best model remains well below observed human performance, so the benchmark is not saturated. Second, every evaluated MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning, showing that recognizing a topological relation does not automatically translate into an executable policy. Third, on Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, SFT followed by RL gives the strongest average performance, but most of the gain appears on reasoning tasks, while planning improves much less. RL also yields uneven transfer to held-out tasks, suggesting limited generalization.

The generative-model-assisted planning experiments are also informative. The paper evaluates agents augmented with image and video generation in the hope that imagined futures or predictive visual rollouts might help action selection. The result is negative in an interesting way: generated observations may preserve local cues and may even look plausible at the endpoint, but audited rollouts do not reliably follow environment dynamics or maintain topological validity across transitions. In other words, visual plausibility is not enough for topology-consistent planning.

The benchmark also appears to support controlled difficulty and property-specific error analysis. While the abstract does not list every score, the full paper should be read for the breakdown by property, cognitive level, and training intervention. The key takeaway is the reasoning–planning gap and the uneven held-out-task gains after training.21:T416,For a quick but useful read, start with the benchmark definition section to understand the five topological properties and the distinction between reasoning and planning. Then jump to the main result figures comparing the 14 MLLMs across both levels. After that, read the Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct training section, focusing on how SFT, RL, and SFT+RL differ on seen versus held-out tasks. Finish with the analysis of planning agents augmented by image/video generation; that section is where the paper clarifies why generative prediction does not automatically yield topology-aware action.

A 20–30 minute skim should be enough to extract the main value. Prioritize benchmark construction, main benchmark results, training interventions, and the generative-planning analysis. Two deeper questions are worth carrying forward: which topological properties require genuinely discrete structural representations rather than visual heuristics, and whether planning failures come mainly from perception, state abstraction, or action-consequence modeling.22:T7e0,The contribution is a benchmark plus a cascading evaluation protocol. The dataset is built from two expert-grounded sources.

The first source is ICLR 2023–2026 OpenReview forums. The authors run a two-stage LLM pipeline to extract novelty-overlap claims from official reviews and author rebuttals, then have three PhD-level annotators verify prior-work identity, overlap dimensions, and reviewer evidence. The released high-confidence subset contains 236 pairs. This source is anchored in real review disputes and tends to emphasize method and problem overlap.

The second source is survey co-citation grouping. Papers grouped together by survey authors as similar are used to construct positives after annotation, while negatives are created by cross-group sampling from distinct similarity groups mentioned within the same sentence. This is a better negative construction than random pairing because it keeps topical proximity while preserving dimension-specific non-overlap. It also contributes more task-level similarity cases.

Each instance is labeled independently along three dimensions: Task, Problem, and Method. These correspond to application goal, core technical challenge, and solution approach. A key design choice is that these are fixed ground-truth labels rather than post hoc interpretations of free-form model outputs. That makes error localization explicit.

The evaluation protocol is stricter than standard benchmark scoring. Models must not only predict overlap per dimension, but also provide verbatim evidence spans. Outputs then go through a three-stage cascade: per-dimension correctness, evidence hallucination check, and reasoning mismatch check. Only predictions that pass all three stages count toward Verified F1. In other words, the benchmark measures “correct answer with faithful support,” not just label coincidence. The benchmark also includes 50 multi-paper grouping sets to test whether models can organize related work more structurally than pairwise comparison alone.23:T486,The benchmark is evaluated on 18 LLMs. The main empirical message is not that one model reaches a particularly high score, but that failure modes are systematic: even when the final overlap judgment is correct, models often support it with fabricated evidence or with evidence that is only weakly connected to the stated conclusion. These failures vary substantially across Task, Problem, and Method dimensions. The paper also notes that the two data sources induce different distributions—ICLR-derived pairs skew toward method/problem overlap, while survey-derived pairs skew toward task similarity—which helps expose dimension-specific weaknesses rather than hiding them under a single distribution.

In terms of scale, the benchmark contains 619 paper pairs and 50 multi-paper sets. The ICLR source starts from 42,682 OpenReview forums, indicating substantial upstream mining before strong human filtering at release time. For readers, the most important quantitative pattern is the gap between ordinary correctness metrics and Verified F1. That gap shows that getting the label right and grounding it faithfully are materially different capabilities.24:T5d2,The paper performs a controlled empirical comparison of six evaluators: HarmBench, JailbreakBench, JailbreakRadar, StrongReject, JADES, and JailMeter. These represent several distinct evaluation paradigms: a dedicated classifier, generic LLM-as-a-judge methods, rubric-based structured judging, decomposition of harmful requests into weighted sub-questions, and evidence-extraction-based evaluation.

The most important methodological choice is variable control. For evaluators that rely on a general-purpose LLM judge, the authors use the same backbone model across methods, so differences can be attributed to evaluation design rather than judge capability. They then evaluate all six methods on two human-labeled datasets, JailbreakQR and JailMeter-Eva, using human annotations as the reference. The analysis covers overall agreement, error types, and consistency across attack families.

A key message from the full paper is that these evaluators are not merely noisy approximations of one common target. They operationalize different notions of jailbreak success. Some are closer to harmful-content detection, others to task fulfillment, and some are more sensitive to superficial compliance or prompt artifacts. JADES gains from decomposing the request before scoring, which reduces interference from irrelevant surface cues. StrongReject benefits from rubric-based structure. HarmBench remains competitive as a dedicated classifier, but its notion of success is more fixed by design.25:T414,The experiments use two human-labeled datasets and compare each evaluator directly against human judgments. The headline result is that JADES performs best overall, with HarmBench and StrongReject also showing strong and relatively stable performance. Other evaluators exhibit larger deviations or more dataset-dependent behavior.

The paper is especially useful because it goes beyond aggregate scores. Its error analysis shows that disagreements are systematic rather than random. Some evaluators over-credit responses that sound harmful but do not actually fulfill the request. Others miss responses that are phrased mildly yet provide actionable harmful assistance. The cross-attack-family analysis also shows that some evaluators are less stable when the jailbreak style changes.

The exact numeric margins are less important than the ranking and the behavioral differences. The paper’s main empirical contribution is a unified comparison framework showing that evaluator choice alone can materially change measured attack success rates.26:T6f9,LoopVAE uses a scale- and loop-conditioned shared core. The encoder and decoder do not share parameters with each other, since analysis and synthesis are different transformations, but within each branch the same fixed-resolution operator is reused across all scales and loop steps. At scale s, a transition module first produces an initial feature h_{s,0}. The shared residual update is then conditioned on a learned scale embedding and a learned loop embedding, and applied L_s times.

A notable design choice is the gated input-injection path. Each scale has a scale-specific projection P_s(h), multiplied by a learnable scalar g_s initialized at zero. The first loop omits this injection; later loops add it once before the whole K-block core. This gives the recurrent process a shortcut back to the feature that entered the current scale, which likely helps prevent drift during repeated refinement. The authors explicitly note that the standalone effect of this path is not isolated experimentally.

The sharing boundary is deliberate. Downsampling, upsampling, and interface projections remain independent because they change tensor geometry and are inherently scale-dependent. Sharing is applied only to the expressive fixed-resolution computation. In the three-scale setup, the model stores K=4 core blocks and uses loop counts L=[1,2,4]. The encoder traverses scales from coarse to fine in one order, while the decoder uses the reverse order, so each branch stores only four blocks but executes 28 block applications. The paper also includes a non-adversarial Transformer ablation that keeps the execution graph fixed while varying the amount of sharing, which is important because it isolates the effect of sharing itself rather than conflating it with backbone differences.27:T63c,The experiments address three questions. First, how much sharing is acceptable? In a roughly 400k-step non-adversarial Transformer setup, the execution graph is held fixed while the number of stored core blocks varies from globally shared to partially shared to fully unshared. Global sharing gives the best PSNR and SSIM, while the fully unshared version gives the best LPIPS. That is a useful nuance: sharing favors pixel-level fidelity and structural consistency, but not necessarily perceptual quality.

Second, the main CNN result: on ImageNet-256, under an approximately 30-epoch two-stage training budget, the final 29M-parameter model reaches 0.28 rFID and 32.54 dB PSNR. The reference local Flux-style VAE uses about 84M parameters, so LoopVAE cuts parameter count by about 65.3% while remaining competitive in reconstruction quality.

Third, are the recurrent passes actually doing useful work, and what do they cost? The paper performs stage truncation and single-pass bypass interventions on trained models. Completing the trained loop schedule consistently improves reconstruction, and even relatively small intermediate feature changes can still produce meaningful output differences. So the repeated computation is not idle. At the same time, truncation results show that intermediate states are not automatically calibrated as an anytime decoder. On efficiency, the 8x-looped CNN stores 65.3% fewer parameters than the reference, but uses 1.57x estimated MACs and 2.85x batch latency. This is arguably the most important practical result: the method saves parameters, not runtime.28:T495,For a quick read, focus on three parts. First, the introduction’s three empirical questions: quality retention, necessity of recurrent depth, and execution cost. They frame the whole paper well. Second, in the method section, pay attention to the exact sharing boundary: the fixed-resolution core is shared, while resolution transitions are not. Also inspect where the scale embedding, loop embedding, and gated input injection enter the computation. That tells you what is actually being reused. Third, in the experiments, prioritize the sharing-scope ablation, the truncation/bypass interventions, and the runtime profiling. These are more informative than the headline reconstruction table alone.

If time is limited, the overview figure, the execution schedule, the Transformer ablation, the recurrent-depth intervention results, and the cost profiling are enough to form a solid judgment in 20–30 minutes. If reading more deeply, two follow-up questions are worth tracking: how much of the gain comes from the input-injection path rather than sharing alone, and whether the same design would transfer to discrete or VQ-style tokenizers rather than continuous VAEs.29:T4d5,The proposed framework is DIAL, or Dual-phase Intrinsic Attention Leveraging. The first component is an analysis of cross-attention in a text-conditioned latent video DiT trained with rectified flow matching. The authors obtain subject masks from a semantic segmentation pipeline and compare them with attention maps across layers and timesteps. They find that a subset of attention blocks consistently produces spatially concentrated maps aligned with the reference subjects. These maps are called ISGM, Intrinsic Spatial Grounding Maps. In late denoising stages, where ISGM is sharp and reliable, DIAL extracts and binarizes the maps into subject-specific spatial guides, then injects them as controllable attention biases into attention blocks. This allows inference-time control of reference fidelity without retraining, including separate control for multiple subjects. In early denoising stages, the maps are noisier, so the paper does not use them as direct hard guidance. Instead, it scores denoising trajectories by ISGM-mask alignment, constructs preference pairs automatically, and applies RL fine-tuning to improve reference grounding. The same internal signal is therefore used differently depending on the denoising regime.2a:Tab1,方法上，ZipCodec 继承 FocalCodec-Stream 的整体五段式结构：encoder / compressor / quantizer / decompressor / decoder，但几乎每一段都重做了。

第一，输入前端从 learned waveform encoder 改成 causal log-mel frontend。具体是 25 ms 窗、10 ms hop、80 维 log-mel，先得到 100 Hz 连续特征，再用 temporal patching 把连续 16 帧拼成一个 patch，直接把帧率降到 6.25 Hz。这样做的取舍很明确：放弃从原始波形端到端学前端，换来更稳定、更轻的流式输入表征，也减少了前端卷积堆栈的复杂度。

第二，主干从 focal modulation 改成 transformer，并提出 ErfFormer。ErfFormer 基本沿用 LLaMA 风格 block：GQA attention + gated FFN + SiLU，但做了两个关键删改。
1) 去掉 normalization，用 DynamicErf 替代 RMSNorm。作者的动机是降低计算和内存开销，同时避免 norm 在流式长时间运行中的一些数值和实现负担。
2) 完全去掉 positional encoding。作者认为连续声学特征本身带局部时序结构，因果注意力已经保留顺序；更重要的是，去掉显式位置后，流式推理不再受训练时上下文长度和位置外推问题约束。这一点对长时在线语音流尤其关键。

第三，量化器采用 scalar spherical quantization, SSQ。做法是先把 compressor 输出投到 L=64 维潜空间，再归一化到单位球面；随后每一维独立量化到 K=4 个标量级别。于是每帧得到 64 个 2-bit symbol，总计 128 bit/frame；乘以 6.25 frame/s，得到 0.80 kbps。这里的核心不是单纯低码率，而是“因子化 bottleneck”：相比单个大码本或多级 VQ，SSQ 更容易扩展、码本利用更均匀，也更适合后续把离散单元当 token 序列处理。

第四，训练目标不是只做波形重建，而是显式蒸馏 WavLM layer-6 表征。作者把大规模 English speech 上的 WavLM 中层表示作为语义-声学折中目标，并复现 WavLM 原始训练中的噪声和重叠说话增强，以缩小 teacher-student 分布差异。选 layer-6 而不是更高层，说明作者希望保留较强语音内容和一定声学细节，而不是过度语义化。

第五，decoder 侧做了 latency-aware streaming 设计，使理论延迟控制在 160 ms。这个数字和 6.25 Hz patch 长度一致，说明整个系统在结构上围绕单 token 覆盖时长来设计，而不是后面再补流式约束。

整体上，这不是“把模型做大”这么简单。ZipCodec 的关键在于把低帧率、流式性、表征蒸馏和可部署性一起联立优化。842M 参数看起来很大，但大头在表征能力和低帧率信息瓶颈补偿，而不是追求离线重建分数。2b:T9c0,ZipCodec keeps the high-level FocalCodec-Stream pipeline—encoder, compressor, quantizer, decompressor, decoder—but redesigns most components.

First, it replaces the learned waveform encoder with a causal log-mel frontend: 80-dim log-mel features with a 25 ms window and 10 ms hop, giving a 100 Hz feature stream. A temporal patching module groups 16 frames and linearly projects them, reducing the rate directly to 6.25 Hz. This is a deliberate trade-off: less end-to-end flexibility, but a simpler and more stable streaming frontend.

Second, it swaps focal modulation for a transformer backbone and introduces ErfFormer. The block is LLaMA-like—grouped-query attention plus gated FFN with SiLU—but with two notable changes: no normalization layers and no positional encodings. RMSNorm is replaced by DynamicErf, motivated by lower compute cost and simpler streaming behavior. Positional encodings are removed entirely. The argument is that continuous acoustic inputs already contain local temporal structure, causal attention preserves order, and removing explicit position dependence avoids extrapolation issues in long-running streaming inference.

Third, the quantizer uses scalar spherical quantization (SSQ). The compressor output is projected to a 64-dimensional latent, normalized to the unit sphere, and each dimension is independently quantized into 4 scalar levels. That yields 64 two-bit symbols per frame, i.e. 128 bits per frame; at 6.25 frames/s this gives 0.80 kbps. The important point is the factorized bottleneck: compared with large codebooks or hierarchical VQ, SSQ is easier to scale and better suited to extremely low frame-rate settings.

Fourth, the training target is not just waveform reconstruction. The model distills WavLM layer-6 representations at scale, using about 94k hours of English speech, and reproduces WavLM-style noise and overlap augmentation to better match the teacher distribution. Choosing an intermediate WavLM layer is meaningful: it preserves a balance between semantic content and acoustic detail.

Fifth, the decoder is explicitly latency-aware, keeping theoretical latency at 160 ms. That aligns with the 160 ms temporal span of each token, showing that streamability is built into the representation rather than patched on later.

Overall, the contribution is not merely scaling model size. The paper jointly optimizes low frame rate, streamability, representational quality, and deployability under a severe information bottleneck.2c:T62a,The key setup details visible from the full text are: 842M parameters, about 94,000 hours of English speech for WavLM distillation, 6.25 Hz frame rate, 0.80 kbps bitrate, and 160 ms theoretical latency. The model is also claimed to run in real time for a single stream on a consumer CPU.

The main empirical claim is that ZipCodec substantially outperforms prior streaming codecs at matched bitrate on both reconstruction and downstream representation tasks, despite operating at a much lower frame rate. The comparisons include streaming baselines such as Mimi, and lower-frame-rate but non-streaming or side-information-based methods such as U-Codec, TaDiCodec, FlexiCodec, and DyCAST. The important result is the Pareto position: ZipCodec improves quality while also reducing frame rate, rather than trading one for the other.

The most relevant ablation takeaways are likely the following:
1) large-scale WavLM distillation and matching augmentation are important for preserving semantic quality at such a low frame rate;
2) the transformer/ErfFormer backbone improves scalability and hardware efficiency over focal modulation;
3) removing positional encodings helps long-running streaming inference rather than merely simplifying the model;
4) SSQ is better suited than conventional VQ under this extreme compression regime.

The provided text does not include the full numeric tables, so it would be unsafe to invent exact benchmark scores. Still, the paper clearly positions the gains as substantial rather than marginal, especially given the stricter frame-rate setting.2d:T731,The system uses three Nemotron-3-Ultra 550B-A55B checkpoints: the general-availability model, an SFT proof specialist, and an RL specialist. The SFT checkpoint is trained from the GA model with a maximum sequence length of 425,984 tokens and the standard per-token cross-entropy objective. The SFT corpus is built by a multi-stage synthetic pipeline. Starting from 15,879 difficult AoPS proof problems, DeepSeek-V4-Pro in Max inference mode generates multiple initial proof attempts with up to 400K tokens. Unsolved problems go through up to three refinement rounds conditioned on earlier attempts and verifier feedback. In parallel, verifier trajectories score candidate proofs with labels in {0, 0.5, 1}, and meta-verifier trajectories judge the reliability of self-evaluations and verifier outputs. The filtering is strict: incomplete, malformed, empty, length-capped, and structurally invalid samples are removed; proof and refinement examples must contain non-empty Solution and Self Evaluation sections; verification examples must contain a parseable final score and evaluate the visible proof. To avoid an SFT distribution dominated by wrong proofs, all valid 0.5 and 1.0 verifier traces are retained, while 0-score traces are deterministically subsampled. The final SFT corpus has 414,890 filtered examples over 15,818 unique problems: 58,543 proof-generation traces, 67,971 refinement traces, 236,360 verification traces, and 52,016 meta-verification traces. The SFT run uses 512 GB200 GPUs with TP=8, CP=32, EP=64, PP=1, global batch size 64, micro-batch size 1, and roughly 1.6K optimizer steps for one pass over packed data. AdamW is used with β1=0.9, β2=0.95, and weight decay 0.1. At inference time, the GA, SFT, and RL checkpoints are assigned to generation, verification, refinement, and final high-compute selection roles.2e:T5ed,Negative Self-Distillation (NSD) has two stages:
1) Self negative conditioning: for each prompt x, the model first generates a per-question negative condition n (e.g., “act as a careless reasoner”). Conditioning on n yields a negative-teacher distribution π_neg from the same base model.
2) Divergence-from-negative with token gating: the student is trained to reduce alignment with π_neg on its sampled trajectory, i.e., push probability mass away from tokens favored by the negatively conditioned teacher.
The core technical issue is selectivity: tokens with high probability under π_neg are not always “reasoning errors”. A naive unlikelihood or negative KL objective would penalize many ordinary structural/language tokens and degrade pretrained linguistic competence. NSD introduces a dynamic token-level gating mechanism using a benign reference model: the negative objective is activated only when the negative condition increases the likelihood of the sampled token relative to the reference. This treats the probability lift induced by the negative condition as a proxy for flawed-reasoning bias.
They further stabilize optimization with bounded unlikelihood (cap the penalty to avoid large gradients on high-confidence common tokens) and a KL regularizer to prevent the student from drifting too far from the reference distribution. Computationally, they emphasize single student rollout per sample, no full-vocabulary logit matching, and parallelizable reference/negative-teacher forward passes.2f:Ta7c,方法很统一：先在一条已生成的 chain 上定义 candidate boundaries，然后在每个 boundary 读取模型此刻对最终答案的“信念”，再用相邻 boundary 之间的 belief change 给前一段打分，选择得分最高的位置 fork。关键细节有两个。其一，作者采用 fork-before attribution：如果 belief 在区间 (t-, t] 发生了明显变化，说明真正起作用的是这一段，因此分数记到 t-，让 siblings 从该步之前重新展开，去估计这一步的 advantage。其二，作者给了三种不同 access level 的 belief readout。

第一种是 PROBE-JS，属于 black-box 方案。做法是在每个 boundary 插入一个很短的 probe，类似“所以你的最终答案是什么”，采样得到 answer distribution，然后计算相邻两个 boundary 的 Jensen-Shannon divergence。JS 大说明模型在这一步前后对最终答案的分布发生了明显变化，可能是关键推理步，也可能是犯错点。它的优点是只需要 sampling access，不依赖内部状态；缺点是每个 boundary 都要额外生成一小段 probe answer。

第二种是 LENS-SHIFT，属于 white-box 方案。它不让模型显式回答，而是用 logit lens 读取各层对最终答案的承诺强度，形成一个 depth profile，再比较相邻 boundary 的 profile 变化幅度。这个设计的意思是：belief shift 不一定只体现在最后一层 logits，也可能体现在整条深度方向上的表征重排。相比 probe，它没有额外 generation cost，但需要访问 hidden states，并且依赖已知 final answer 作为 readout target。

第三种是 BSV（Belief-Shift Vector）。作者先离线收集高置信与犹豫状态的 activation，对比拟合出一个“confidence shift”方向，之后只需把 boundary hidden state 投影到这个方向上即可打分。它更像一个 amortized readout，用很低成本近似直接测量 belief shift。论文里它主要用于 pre-RL validation，验证如果能更直接地读到 belief shift，是否会更贴近 Monte-Carlo ground-truth value curve。也就是说，BSV 更像分析工具，而不是主训练时的标准配置。

与现有方法相比，差异很明确。固定网格、midpoint、delimiter 这类结构式方法完全不看该步是否决定 outcome；entropy/surprisal 方法看的是局部 token 不确定性，容易被措辞自由度、同义改写、代码表面分支等因素放大，却不一定对应最终答案会不会变。作者强调，belief-shift 关注的是 answer belief 的变化，而不是 next-token freedom，这个目标更接近 tree RLVR 真正需要的“高价值分叉点”。30:T75c,实验分成两层。第一层是 RL 前的 selector 诊断。作者用 Monte-Carlo 估计 ground-truth value curve，把不同 selector 选中的 fork 点与真实 value movement 对齐，衡量谁更能抓住关键转折。文中给出的代表性例子里，在相同 10 个 fork 预算下，Probe-JS 捕获的总 value movement 是 entropy 的 3.6 倍，数值为 1.56 对 0.44；entropy 有 6 个 fork 浪费在 V*=1 的 settled region。更重要的是，belief-shift 信号在 8 个 model×benchmark panel 中都排第一，超过 entropy、结构式 baseline 和 LLM-judge baseline，并且能 zero-shot 转移到 out-of-domain benchmark。这个结果说明它不是只在某个模型或某个任务模板上偶然有效。

第二层是实际 RL 训练。作者在 matched budget 下比较四个只差 fork criterion 的训练臂，覆盖三类模型家族、数学和代码两个领域。摘要和正文给出的关键结果是：belief-shift 分叉在所有数学 aggregate 上领先，在 OLMo-3-7B 的 AIME 2026 上比最强 baseline 高 2.9 分；在 OLMo 代码实验的所有列上也全部领先。虽然这里没有给出每一列完整数字，但从描述看，收益不是单点偶然，而是跨 domain、跨 family 都比较稳定。

消融做得也比较完整。作者检查了 tree budget (M, k)、policy updater、probe read length L、Lens-Shift 的 readout 方式，以及模型规模从 Qwen3-4B 到 Qwen3-8B 的变化。一个实用信息是 probe 成本：在数学任务上约占一个 training step 的 1%，在代码上低于 5%，前提是 probe 由 rollout engine 一并服务。这个成本不算零，但相对 tree rollout 本身的 token 开销是可接受的。论文还强调，selector 只影响 fork placement，不直接改 reward，因此 calibration 不完美时主要损失 sample efficiency，而不是引入系统性错误梯度。31:T413,This is not a pretraining-objective paper, but it matters at the pretrain-to-post-train interface because it improves the quality of RL credit signals for reasoning models trained with RLVR. Its contribution is useful in three ways. First, it cleanly separates two roles that are often conflated in tree RL: the tree structure converts sibling outcomes into step credit, while fork placement determines whether those credits are informative. Second, the belief-shift view is more aligned with “which step actually changed the model’s answer belief” than entropy-based uncertainty, which is a meaningful design principle for reasoning-trace supervision. Third, the method is relatively safe: belief signals are used only to choose fork locations, not to define rewards, so miscalibration mostly wastes budget rather than corrupting optimization. In scale, this looks like a solid training-recipe improvement rather than a new learning paradigm, but if tree RLVR becomes standard, fork policy could become a default component of the stack.32:Ta8d,The framework is simple and consistent. For a generated chain, define candidate boundaries. At each boundary, read the model’s current belief about the final answer. Score each segment by the amount of belief change between consecutive boundaries, and fork at the highest-scoring location. Two details matter.

First, the paper uses fork-before attribution. If belief changes sharply over segment (t-, t], the causal step is that segment, so the score is assigned to t- and sibling continuations are re-rolled from just before the belief-moving step. This aligns the counterfactual branch with the step whose contribution is being estimated.

Second, the paper instantiates belief readout at three access levels.

PROBE-JS is the black-box version. At each boundary, the model is interrupted with a short probe such as “so what is your final answer?”, and the resulting answer distribution is sampled. The score is the Jensen–Shannon divergence between consecutive boundary distributions. Large JS indicates that the segment caused a meaningful shift in answer belief. The advantage is that it only requires sampling access; the downside is extra generation at every boundary.

LENS-SHIFT is the white-box version. Instead of making the model answer explicitly, it uses a logit-lens style readout to measure how strongly each layer already commits to the final answer, producing a depth profile at each boundary. The score is the change in this profile across adjacent boundaries. The motivation is that belief shifts may appear as representational reorganization across depth, not only as a change in final logits. This avoids extra generation but requires hidden-state access and a target final answer for readout.

BSV, or Belief-Shift Vector, is an amortized readout. The authors fit an activation direction offline by contrasting high-confidence and doubtful states, then score a boundary by projecting its hidden state onto that direction. In the paper, BSV is mainly used for pre-RL validation: if one could measure belief shift more directly, would it align better with the Monte-Carlo value curve? So BSV functions more as an analysis instrument than the default online training method.

The distinction from prior selectors is sharp. Fixed grids, midpoints, and delimiter-based rules ignore whether a step actually changes the outcome. Entropy or surprisal tracks local token uncertainty, which is often inflated by paraphrase freedom, stylistic variation, or surface branching in code, without implying any change in final answer. Belief-shift branching instead targets answer-belief movement, which is much closer to the quantity tree RLVR actually needs: high-value branch points.33:T7cf,The evaluation has two layers. The first is pre-RL selector diagnosis. Using Monte-Carlo estimates of the ground-truth value curve, the paper measures how much true value movement is captured by the selected fork points. In the representative example highlighted early in the paper, with the same budget of 10 forks, Probe-JS captures 3.6× more total value movement than entropy: 1.56 versus 0.44. Entropy wastes 6 of its 10 forks in a settled region where V* is already identically 1. More broadly, a belief-shift signal ranks first in all 8 model-by-benchmark panels, outperforming entropy, structural baselines, and an LLM-judge baseline, and it transfers zero-shot to an out-of-domain benchmark. That is important because it suggests the signal is not tied to one model family or one task template.

The second layer is actual RL training. Under matched rollout budgets, the authors compare four training arms that differ only in the fork criterion, spanning three model families and two domains: mathematics and code. The headline result reported in the text is that belief-shift branching leads every mathematics aggregate and improves OLMo-3-7B by +2.9 on AIME 2026 over the strongest baseline. It also wins every OLMo code column. Even without all table entries reproduced in the prompt, the pattern described is broad rather than anecdotal.

The ablations are reasonably thorough. The paper varies the tree budget (M, k), the policy updater, the probe read length L, the Lens-Shift readout, and model scale from Qwen3-4B to Qwen3-8B. One practical number stands out: probe overhead is about 1% of a training step on math and under 5% on code when served by the rollout engine. That is not free, but relative to the cost of tree rollouts it is manageable. The paper also makes an important robustness point: because the selector only affects fork placement and not reward definition, imperfect calibration mainly hurts sample efficiency rather than injecting systematically wrong gradients.34:T4c0,For a quick read, start with the Introduction and Figure 1. They make the central objection to entropy-based forking very concrete: entropy can be high where value movement is negligible, and low where the model’s answer belief actually flips. Then read Section 3 carefully, especially the access assumptions of the three readouts, the scoring rule, and the fork-before attribution detail. That is where the method becomes more than a heuristic. After that, go to Section 4 to verify that the authors do not rely only on end-task RL scores; they first test whether the selector tracks the value curve itself. Finish with Sections 6 and 7 for downstream RL gains and recipe robustness.

If you only have 20–30 minutes, the efficient path is: Introduction → Figure 1 → Sections 3.2/3.3/3.4 → Section 4 result figures/tables → Section 6 main RL table. If you want to dig deeper, three questions are worth keeping in mind: whether belief-shift is biased toward tasks with explicit answer flips, how answer distributions are defined for multi-solution or format-unstable tasks, and whether Lens-Shift or BSV can be turned into a low-cost online policy rather than remaining an analysis-time or white-box tool.35:T628,The method is Distribution-aware Language Neuron selection (DLN). For each MLP neuron in an autoregressive Transformer with GLU feed-forward blocks, the authors collect continuous activation values for each language instead of reducing them to a binary active/inactive statistic. They then estimate per-language activation distributions and compute pairwise overlap coefficients between languages. Small overlap means the neuron separates those languages well. The resulting pairwise structure is clustered to recover language groupings.

This leads to two neuron types. Single-language neurons (SLNs) are neurons whose activation distribution for one language is separated from the rest. Multi-language neurons (MLNs) are neurons that support a cluster of languages that are similar to each other but separated from other languages. This is a broader and more faithful definition than entropy over positive activation rates, because it captures cases where all languages are mostly positive but occupy different regions, and cases where the informative regime is negative.

A useful detail is the intervention site. The paper evaluates both the usual gate activation site and the gate-up product site, the latter being closer to the effective contribution written back to the residual stream in GLU blocks. Baselines based on activation probability degrade substantially there, while DLN remains selective. The main innovation is therefore not a heavy model component, but a better neuron selection criterion built on continuous distributions and pairwise language relations.36:T4dc,Main experiments are conducted on Llama-3.1-8B and SmolLM3-3B-Base using two held-out corpora, with three additional models in the appendix. The key evaluation is causal ablation rather than correlation-style probing: remove selected neurons and measure target-language damage versus collateral damage on other languages.

For SLNs, DLN yields up to 4.9x higher on-target language damage per neuron while preserving off-target performance better than activation-probability baselines. This suggests the selected neurons are more causally specific rather than merely important in a generic sense. For MLNs, DLN uncovers shared structures such as a Chinese–Japanese coalition, producing 7.12x greater within-cluster damage while using 2.1x fewer neurons.

Two analyses stand out. First, DLN finds English SLNs in the negative activation regime that are invisible to positive-rate metrics but still act as functional triggers for language control. Second, DLN remains effective at the gate-up product site, where baseline methods lose much of their selectivity. The strongest evidence in the paper is not an average benchmark gain, but improved causal selectivity: fewer neurons, stronger target-specific effects, and less off-target disruption.37:T4d7,Two-layer contribution:
- Theory (MUtE*): define erasure as a map f: R^D→R^D producing Q=f(X) such that concept information is removed (perfect privacy / indistinguishability across class-conditionals in the erased space) while utility is maximized (minimal distortion / maximal retained information under the chosen criterion). The authors characterize optimal erasure functions and show that optimality inherently induces a dual deterministic counterfactual mapping between concept classes.
- Practice (MUtE): to avoid the heavy compute of general nonlinear erasure (adversarial training, density estimation, Bayesian optimization), they propose an implementation with a translational bias: many concepts in modern encoders behave approximately like linear directions / translations, so counterfactual trajectories can be parameterized as (near) translations. This yields an efficient procedure that supports both erasure and counterfactual representation generation.
Compared to projection-based linear methods (INLP/LEACE), MUtE expands the objective to a more general optimality framing and explicitly couples erasure with counterfactuals; computationally, it trades generality for scalability via a structured geometric assumption.38:T605,The method is straightforward. For each trained model, the authors extract WeightWatcher metrics from layer weight matrices, focusing on quantities such as stable rank and log α-norm, alongside more conventional measures of generalization. The theoretical backdrop is heavy-tailed self-regularization, where well-trained networks are expected to deviate from random-matrix behavior and exhibit structured empirical spectral densities. WeightWatcher summarizes this structure through power-law fits and norm-based statistics.

The empirical protocol then compares these spectral metrics against actual MIA vulnerability measured by attacks such as LiRA. The analysis separates overall attack success from performance in the low-false-positive-rate regime, which matters more for practical auditing. The main finding is that stable rank is strongly positively correlated with overall MIA success, while log α-norm is consistently negatively correlated with attack success in the low-FPR regime. The paper also shows that spectral metrics and generalization gap are not redundant: combining them improves prediction of whether a model should be considered high risk.

A useful nuance from the full paper is that the authors do not claim spectral metrics explain privacy leakage mechanistically. Their position is more modest. These metrics appear to capture aspects of internal organization that are missed by train-test gap alone, but the work stops short of linking layerwise spectral evolution to sample-level memorization in a causal way.39:T58b,The experiments are conducted on image and tabular classification models rather than language models, which is an important limitation. Across multiple datasets and model settings, the paper compares WeightWatcher metrics, generalization gap, and measured MIA leakage. Three results stand out.

First, stable rank is a stronger predictor of overall MIA success than generalization gap across the evaluated settings. This matters if the goal is coarse screening of which trained models are more likely to leak membership information.

Second, in the low-FPR regime, log α-norm is a more consistent predictor than generalization gap. That is a practically relevant result because privacy auditing usually cares about low false-positive operation.

Third, spectral metrics and generalization gap provide complementary information. A combined predictor performs better at identifying high-risk models than either signal alone. The paper also notes that not all WeightWatcher metrics are equally useful; stable rank and log α-norm are the most reliable among those tested.

The practical advantage is computational: unlike LiRA or RMIA, the proposed proxy only needs access to trained weights. The limitations are equally clear: no Transformer or LLM experiments, no large-scale pretraining setting, and no evidence yet that the correlations remain stable across optimizer choices, training scales, or distribution shifts.3a:T414,For a skim, start with the introduction and background to understand what is being replaced: expensive shadow-model-based privacy auditing, not privacy evaluation as a whole. Then look at the definitions of stable rank, α, and log α-norm, because the paper’s conclusions depend on these metrics behaving differently rather than as one generic notion of spectral complexity.

In the experiments, focus on three pieces: the correlation plots between spectral metrics and MIA success, the comparison against generalization gap, and the binary high-risk identification results using combined features. Pay attention to the distinction between overall MIA success and low-FPR behavior; much of the practical value comes from that split.

This is a roughly 30-minute skim. The deeper questions worth carrying forward are whether the same signals hold for Transformers and LLM checkpoints, and why stable rank correlates positively with leakage risk in these settings. 的分解结果，因为这关系到真实预训练中如何安排 data mixture 和 epoching。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper studies a practical pretraining failure mode: what happens to sparse MoE language models when the training corpus is repeated because unique human-written data is limited. Prior data-constrained scaling work mostly focused on dense Transformers, while MoE scaling work often assumed abundant unique data. This paper puts the two axes together: repetition separates total tokens from unique tokens, and sparsity separates active parameters from total parameters.","importance":"The main implication is that the compute advantage of MoEs is conditional on the amount of unique data. With enough unique data, MoEs retain their advantage over dense models. Under high repetition, they overfit earlier and more sharply. The degradation tracks total parameters more than active parameters, which is important because MoE training recipes are usually compute-matched by active parameters. For pretraining, this means that expert count, data repetition, deduplication, domain mixture, and regularization should be tuned jointly rather than treated as independent choices.","technical_approach":"$a","experiments":"$b","reading_guide":"Read the experimental setup carefully before the results. The important design choice is the separation between total tokens and unique tokens, and between active and total parameters. The main result section is useful for understanding where dense and MoE curves cross as repetition increases. The mixed-domain experiments are especially relevant for real pretraining, because repetition is rarely uniform across domains. The regularization section is practical: dropout and output masking give concrete ways to recover part of the MoE advantage under data scarcity. The mechanistic section is the most diagnostic part of the paper. Those counts ","starred":false,"priority":"p2","reading_tier":"must_read","source":"arxiv","sources":["arxiv"],"published_at":"","category_id":"pretraining_data","cover_url":"/covers/2609_10830.png","hf_upvotes":0,"arxiv_categories":["cs.CL","cs.CR","cs.LG"],"summary":{"core_problem":"论文要回答一个更精确的 membership inference 问题：当训练语料中的句子重复次数是已知的，语言模型 loss 中到底在多少拷贝数之后才出现可检测的训练暴露信号，以及这个信号能否和句子本身的流畅性、名气、文体差异分开。","importance":"这篇工作对预训练数据研究和隐私评估很有用，因为它把“某句子是否在训练集中”从二元标签改成了“暴露剂量”。已有 membership inference 评测常常需要猜测 member 和 non-member，容易把时间、来源、文体、质量差异误当成记忆信号。论文使用 OLMo-2 和 Pythia 的公开预训练语料，以及 infini-gram 的精确句子计数，直接测量拷贝数和 loss 的关系。核心结论很克制：普通文本在 1 到 1000 次拷贝范围内，loss token 序列的精确出现次数。这个计数是 exact-match count，因此是暴露下界：标点、换行、引号形式变化都会导致不计入；OLMo-2 额外 annealing 阶段的数据未被索引覆盖，也使计数偏保守。论文设计了几类实验。第一类按拷贝数分桶，直接看 loss-based detector 在普通句子和高重复名句上的 AUC。第二类是 same-sentence, two-model design：同一句子分别用 OLMo-2 和 Pythia 读，比较哪个模型所在语料中该句子出现更多，以及该模型是否给出更低 loss。因为句子完全相同，流畅性、长度、作者表达质量被固定住；比较只剩语料暴露差异。第三类分析常见 non-member 构造方式的偏差，尤其是一词替换 edit control。第四类替换不同文体的 control，展示 register mismatch 可以显著抬高检测器 AUC。这个设计的重点不是提出更强攻击，而是拆解 loss 信号中哪些来自训练暴露，哪些来自文本质量和评测集构造。","experiments":"实验覆盖 OLMo-2 和 Pythia 两个模型族，参数规模从 1B 到 13B。按拷贝数看，1 到 1000 次范围内 loss-based membership 基本在噪声附近；在普通句子上，1B 模型检测 AUC 约为 0.60；对中位拷贝数约 1200 的名句，AUC 达到 0.83。same-sentence 设计使用 747 个句子，并在同一本书内排序比较，7B 模型的 rank correlation 约为 −0.08，13B 约为 −0.07；−1 表示暴露越多 loss 越低的完美关系，0 表示无关系，因此这个效应解释不到 1% 方差，并且没有随模型变大而增强。一词替换实验显示，超过 90% 的 pair 中原句 loss 低于 edit，平均差距约 0.4 nats/token，也就是原句 token 平均概率约为 edit 的 1.5 倍；但这个差距在原句出现 1 次和 100 次时基本相同，说明模型奖励的是作者原始措辞和文本自然度，而不是训练记忆。超过 1000 次拷贝后，在可测的 12 个句子上，该 gap 会随模型规模增长。文体控制实验显示，在相同 member 句子上，把 same-source edits 换成 composed-prose controls，检测 AUC 从 0.83 升到 0.94，说明 register mismatch 本身就能制造很强的 apparent membership signal。","reading_guide":"建议精读 exposure counting 和 same-sentence design 两部分。先看 消融。","authors_note":""},"summary_en":{"core_problem":"The paper studies when language-model loss provides real evidence of training exposure, given verified duplication counts. It asks how many exact copies are needed before exposure is detectable, whether that signal can be separated from sentence quality and fame, and how much reported membership-inference performance is produced by biased control construction.","importance":"The contribution is methodological and directly relevant to pretraining-data auditing. Instead of treating membership as a guessed binary label, the paper treats it as an observed exposure dose. This matters because web-scale corpora contain many repeated sentences, and loss-based attacks can easily confuse memorization with fluency, source, date, register, or cultural familiarity. By using public pretraining corpora for OLMo-2 and Pythia together with exact infini-gram counts, the paper provides a cleaner measurement of what loss can and cannot reveal. The main implication is that ordinary duplication levels leave only a weak trace in loss, while strong signals appear mostly for very frequent, famous sentences where exposure is confounded across corpora. This is useful for membership-inference evaluation, deduplication policy, and claims about privacy leakage from base models.","technical_approach":"$c","experiments":"$d","reading_guide":"Read the counting section first. The exact-match definition, the floor nature of the counts, the zero-count checks, and the missing OLMo annealing data are important for interpreting every result. Then read the same-sentence two-model design, which is the cleanest part of the paper because it fixes the sentence and varies exposure across corpora. Figure 6 and Section 8 are the key synthesis points: they explain why exposure is detectable mainly where it is also confounded by fame, while corpus disagreements occur mostly where the signal is weak. The control experiments are also worth reading, especially the one-word edit analysis and the register-control swap.
